## Law and government

Main article: [Government of Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Indiana "Government of Indiana")

See also: [Indiana's congressional delegations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana's_congressional_delegations "Indiana's congressional delegations") and [Indiana's congressional districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana's_congressional_districts "Indiana's congressional districts")

[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b3/IndianaStateHouse1.jpg/250px-IndianaStateHouse1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:IndianaStateHouse1.jpg)

The [Indiana Statehouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Statehouse "Indiana Statehouse") in [Indianapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianapolis "Indianapolis"), seat of [Indiana's state government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Indiana "Government of Indiana"), hosts the [Indiana General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_General_Assembly "Indiana General Assembly"), the [Indiana Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Supreme_Court "Indiana Supreme Court"), and the [Governor of Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Indiana "Governor of Indiana").

 [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/1816_Indiana_Constitution_-_DPLA_-_638529f8d3cb7f846cb4d281a6314e78_%28page_10%29.jpg/250px-1816_Indiana_Constitution_-_DPLA_-_638529f8d3cb7f846cb4d281a6314e78_%28page_10%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:1816_Indiana_Constitution_-_DPLA_-_638529f8d3cb7f846cb4d281a6314e78_(page_10).jpg)

[Constitution of Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Indiana "Constitution of Indiana") (1816)

Indiana has a constitutional democratic republican form of government with three branches: the executive, including an elected governor and lieutenant governor; the legislative, consisting of an elected [bicameral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameralism "Bicameralism") General Assembly; and the judicial, the Supreme Court of Indiana, the Indiana Court of Appeals and circuit courts.

The [Governor of Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Indiana "Governor of Indiana") serves as the state's chief executive and has the authority to manage the government as established in the Constitution of Indiana. The governor and the [lieutenant governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_of_Indiana "Lieutenant Governor of Indiana") are jointly elected to four-year terms, with gubernatorial elections running concurrently with United States presidential elections (1996, 2000, 2004, 2008, etc.).[[182]](#cite_note-ST-191) The governor may not serve more than two consecutive terms.[[182]](#cite_note-ST-191) The governor works with the [Indiana General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_General_Assembly "Indiana General Assembly") and the [Indiana Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Supreme_Court "Indiana Supreme Court") to govern the state and has the authority to adjust the other branches. The governor can call special sessions of the General Assembly and select and remove leaders of nearly all state departments, boards and commissions. Other notable powers include calling out the [Indiana Guard Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Guard_Reserve "Indiana Guard Reserve") or the [Indiana National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_National_Guard "Indiana National Guard") in times of emergency or disaster, issuing pardons or commuting the sentence of any criminal offenders except in cases of treason or impeachment and possessing an abundant amount of statutory authority.[[182]](#cite_note-ST-191)[[183]](#cite_note-192)[[184]](#cite_note-193)

The lieutenant governor serves as the President of the Senate and ensures the senate rules are acted in accordance with by its constituents. The lieutenant governor votes only when needed to break ties. If the governor dies in office, becomes permanently incapacitated, resigns or is impeached, the lieutenant governor becomes governor. If both the governor and lieutenant governor positions are unoccupied, the Senate President pro tempore becomes governor.[[185]](#cite_note-194)

The Indiana General Assembly is composed of a 50-member [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Senate "Indiana Senate") and 100-member [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_House_of_Representatives "Indiana House of Representatives"). The Senate is the [upper house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_house "Upper house") of the General Assembly and the House of Representatives is the [lower house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_house "Lower house").[[182]](#cite_note-ST-191) The General Assembly has exclusive legislative authority within the state government. Both the Senate and the House can introduce legislation, with the exception that the Senate is not authorized to initiate legislation that will affect revenue. Bills are debated and passed separately in each house, but both houses must pass them before they can be submitted to the Governor.[[186]](#cite_note-For-195) The legislature can nullify a veto from the governor with a majority vote of full membership in the Senate and House of Representatives.[[182]](#cite_note-ST-191) Each law passed by the General Assembly must apply without exception to the entire state. The General Assembly has no authority to create legislation that targets a particular community.[[186]](#cite_note-For-195)[[187]](#cite_note-196) The General Assembly can manage the state's judiciary system by arranging the size of the courts and the bounds of their districts. It also can oversee the activities of the executive branch of the state government, has restricted power to regulate the county governments within the state, and has exclusive power to initiate the method to alter the Indiana Constitution.[[186]](#cite_note-For-195)[[188]](#cite_note-197)

The [Indiana Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Supreme_Court "Indiana Supreme Court") is made up of five judges with a [Court of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Court_of_Appeals "Indiana Court of Appeals") composed of 15 judges. The governor selects judges for the supreme and appeals courts from a group of applicants chosen by a special commission. After serving for two years, the judges must acquire the support of the electorate to serve for a 10-year term.[[182]](#cite_note-ST-191) In nearly all cases, the Supreme Court does not have [original jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Original_jurisdiction "Original jurisdiction") and can hear only cases petitioned to it after being heard in lower courts. Local circuit courts are where most cases begin with a trial and the consequence is decided by the jury. The Supreme Court has original and sole jurisdiction in certain areas including the practice of law, discipline or disbarment of Judges appointed to the lower state courts, and supervision over the exercise of jurisdiction by the other lower courts of the State.[[189]](#cite_note-Fur-198)[[190]](#cite_note-199)

The state is divided into 92 [counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_(United_States) "County (United States)"), which are led by a board of county commissioners. 90 counties in Indiana have their own [circuit court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circuit_court "Circuit court") with a judge elected for a six-year term. The remaining two counties, Dearborn and Ohio, are combined into one circuit. Many counties operate [superior courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superior_court "Superior court") in addition to the circuit court. In densely populated counties where the caseload is traditionally greater, separate courts have been established to solely hear either juvenile, criminal, probate or small claims cases. The establishment, frequency and jurisdiction of these additional courts vary greatly from county to county. There are 85 city and town courts in Indiana municipalities, created by local ordinance, typically handling minor offenses and not considered [courts of record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courts_of_record "Courts of record"). County officials elected to four-year terms include an auditor, recorder, treasurer, sheriff, coroner and clerk of the circuit court. All incorporated cities in Indiana have a mayor and council form of municipal government. Towns are governed by a town council and townships are governed by a township trustee and advisory board.[[182]](#cite_note-ST-191)[[191]](#cite_note-200)

*[U.S. News & World Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._News_&_World_Report "U.S. News & World Report")* ranked Indiana first in the publication's inaugural 2017 Best States for Government listing. Among individual categories, Indiana ranked above average in budget transparency (#1), government digitization (#6), and fiscal stability (#8), and ranked average in state integrity (#25).[[192]](#cite_note-201)

In a 2020 study, Indiana was ranked as the 10th hardest state for citizens to vote in.[[193]](#cite_note-202) [Abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Indiana "Abortion in Indiana") is illegal in Indiana with limited exceptions.[[194]](#cite_note-203)

In 2015, Indiana expanded Medicaid eligibility from ACA provisions. A study from Kaiser Family Foundation found that the uninsured rate in Indiana dropped from 14% in 2014 to 8.5% in 2015 after the expansion. Under Obamacare, when a state adopts Medicaid expansion, the eligibility income requirement goes from 100% of the federal poverty line to 138% of the federal poverty rate. However, Indiana requires enrollees to contribute to a "POWER account" which functions as a health savings account for all beneficiaries.[[195]](#cite_note-204) This has been controversial because beneficiaries would have to pay into an account with money they would not have.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

### Military installations

[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Defense.gov_photo_essay_090826-D-8719J-45.jpg/250px-Defense.gov_photo_essay_090826-D-8719J-45.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Defense.gov_photo_essay_090826-D-8719J-45.jpg)

Members of the [Indiana National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_National_Guard "Indiana National Guard") at the [Muscatatuck Urban Training Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscatatuck_Urban_Training_Center "Muscatatuck Urban Training Center") near [Butlerville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butlerville,_Indiana "Butlerville, Indiana")

Indiana is home to several current and former [military installations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_base "Military base"). The largest of these is the [Naval Surface Warfare Center Crane Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Surface_Warfare_Center_Crane_Division "Naval Surface Warfare Center Crane Division"), approximately 25 miles southwest of [Bloomington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomington,_Indiana "Bloomington, Indiana"), which is the third-largest naval installation in the world, comprising approximately 108 square miles of territory.[[196]](#cite_note-205) Located inside NSWC Crane is Constitution Grove, a 64,000-acre forest containing approximately 120 old-growth [white oak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_oak "White oak") trees selected for the maintenance of the [USS Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Constitution "USS Constitution"), the world's oldest commissioned warship still afloat.[[197]](#cite_note-206)

Other active installations include [Air National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_National_Guard "Air National Guard") fighter units at [Fort Wayne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Wayne,_Indiana "Fort Wayne, Indiana"), and [Terre Haute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terre_Haute "Terre Haute") airports (to be consolidated at Fort Wayne under the [2005 BRAC proposal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Base_Realignment_and_Closure,_2005 "Base Realignment and Closure, 2005"), with the Terre Haute facility remaining open as a non-flying installation). The [Army National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_National_Guard "Army National Guard") conducts operations at [Camp Atterbury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Atterbury "Camp Atterbury") in [Edinburgh, Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edinburgh,_Indiana "Edinburgh, Indiana"), helicopter operations out of [Shelbyville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shelbyville,_Indiana "Shelbyville, Indiana") Airport and urban training at [Muscatatuck Urban Training Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscatatuck_Urban_Training_Center "Muscatatuck Urban Training Center"). The Army's [Newport Chemical Depot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newport_Chemical_Depot "Newport Chemical Depot"), which is now closed and turning into a coal purifier plant.

Indiana was formerly home to two major military installations; [Grissom Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grissom_Air_Force_Base "Grissom Air Force Base") near Peru (realigned to an [Air Force Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Force_Reserve "Air Force Reserve") installation in 1994) and [Fort Benjamin Harrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Benjamin_Harrison "Fort Benjamin Harrison") near Indianapolis, now closed, though the [Department of Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Defense "United States Department of Defense") continues to operate a large finance center there ([Defense Finance and Accounting Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_Finance_and_Accounting_Service "Defense Finance and Accounting Service")).

## Politics

Main article: [Politics of Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Indiana "Politics of Indiana")

See also: [Political party strength in Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_party_strength_in_Indiana "Political party strength in Indiana") and [United States presidential elections in Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_elections_in_Indiana "United States presidential elections in Indiana")

[![An older man in a tan suit reaches across a table to shake a woman's hand.](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/130410-Z-9531S-013.jpg/250px-130410-Z-9531S-013.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:130410-Z-9531S-013.jpg)

[Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence "Mike Pence") at the Indiana State Fair, 2014

From 1880 to 1924, a resident of Indiana was included in all but one presidential election. Indiana Representative [William Hayden English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Hayden_English "William Hayden English") was nominated for vice president and ran with Winfield Scott Hancock in the [1880 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1880_United_States_presidential_election "1880 United States presidential election").[[198]](#cite_note-207) Former Indiana Governor [Thomas A. Hendricks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_A._Hendricks "Thomas A. Hendricks") was elected vice president in 1884. He served until his death on November 25, 1885, under President [Grover Cleveland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grover_Cleveland "Grover Cleveland").[[199]](#cite_note-208) In 1888, former Senator from Indiana [Benjamin Harrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Harrison "Benjamin Harrison") was elected president and served one term. He remains the only President from Indiana. Indiana Senator [Charles W. Fairbanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_W._Fairbanks "Charles W. Fairbanks") was elected vice president in 1904, serving under President [Theodore Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt "Theodore Roosevelt") until 1909.[[200]](#cite_note-209) Fairbanks made another run for vice president with [Charles Evans Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Evans_Hughes "Charles Evans Hughes") in 1916, but they both lost to [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson "Woodrow Wilson") and former Indiana Governor [Thomas R. Marshall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_R._Marshall "Thomas R. Marshall"), who served as vice president from 1913 until 1921.[[201]](#cite_note-210) Not until 1988 did another presidential election involve a native of Indiana when Senator [Dan Quayle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Quayle "Dan Quayle") was elected vice president and served one term with [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush "George H. W. Bush").[[93]](#cite_note-FW-96) Governor [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence "Mike Pence") was elected vice president in 2016 and served one term with [Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump").

Indiana has long been considered a [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States) "Republican Party (United States)") stronghold.[[202]](#cite_note-211)[[203]](#cite_note-212) It was through the Republican Party that in the mid-1920s the 250,000 member [Indiana Klan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Klan "Indiana Klan") exerted its influence in the [Indiana General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_General_Assembly "Indiana General Assembly"), where more than half the elected representatives were Klansmen, and in the governor's office.[[64]](#cite_note-Moore-1991-67) The party suffered accordingly when the [Madge Oberholtzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madge_Oberholtzer "Madge Oberholtzer") scandal discredited the Klan leadership.[[65]](#cite_note-Lutholtz-1991-68) A further opening was created for the Democrats when the state shared in the country's disillusion with the [Depression-era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression "Great Depression") administration of [President Hoover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Hoover "Herbert Hoover"). In 1932, [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt "Franklin D. Roosevelt") won the state with 55% of the vote. The ascendancy of the [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal "New Deal") Democrats, however, was temporary. In 1940, Indiana was one of only 10 states to support Republican [Wendell Willkie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wendell_Willkie "Wendell Willkie").[[93]](#cite_note-FW-96)

On 14 occasions the Republican candidate has defeated the Democrat by a double-digit margin in the state, including six times where a Republican won the state by more than 20 percentage points.[[204]](#cite_note-Atlas-213) Roosevelt's victory in 1932 was one of only five occasion since 1900 in which the state has favored a [Democrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States) "Democratic Party (United States)") for president. The first was [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson "Woodrow Wilson") with 43% of the vote in 1912; the last, by a narrow margin of 50% to 49%, was [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama "Barack Obama") in 2008.[[205]](#cite_note-214)

Most recently, Republican [Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump") carried the state in [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election_in_Indiana "2016 United States presidential election in Indiana"), [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Indiana "2020 United States presidential election in Indiana"), and [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_States_presidential_election_in_Indiana "2024 United States presidential election in Indiana").

While only five Democratic presidential nominees have carried Indiana since 1900, 11 Democrats [were elected governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Governors_of_Indiana "List of Governors of Indiana") during that time. Before [Mitch Daniels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitch_Daniels "Mitch Daniels") became governor in 2005, Democrats had held the office for 16 consecutive years. Indiana elects two senators and nine representatives to Congress. The state has 11 electoral votes in presidential elections.[[204]](#cite_note-Atlas-213) Seven of the [districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana's_congressional_districts "Indiana's congressional districts") favor the Republican Party according to the CPVI rankings; there are seven Republicans serving as representatives and two Democrats.

Historically, Republicans have been strongest in the eastern and central portions of the state, while Democrats have been strongest in the northwestern part of the state. Occasionally, certain counties in the southern part of the state will vote Democratic. [Marion County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marion_County,_Indiana "Marion County, Indiana"), Indiana's most populous county, supported the Republican candidates from 1968 to 2000, before backing the Democrats in the 2004, 2008, 2012, 2016, 2020, and 2024 elections. Indiana's second-most populous county, [Lake County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_County,_Indiana "Lake County, Indiana"), strongly supports the Democratic party and has not voted for a Republican since 1972.[[204]](#cite_note-Atlas-213)

In 2005, the Bay Area Center for Voting Research rated the most liberal and conservative cities in the United States on voting statistics in the 2004 presidential election, based on 237 cities with populations of more than 100,000. Five Indiana cities were mentioned in the study. On the liberal side, [Gary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary,_Indiana "Gary, Indiana") was ranked second and [South Bend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Bend,_Indiana "South Bend, Indiana") came in at 83. Among conservative cities, [Fort Wayne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Wayne,_Indiana "Fort Wayne, Indiana") was 44th, [Evansville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evansville,_Indiana "Evansville, Indiana") was 60th and [Indianapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianapolis "Indianapolis") was 82nd on the list.[[206]](#cite_note-215) Republicans also currently hold [supermajorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermajority "Supermajority") in both chambers of the [Indiana General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_General_Assembly "Indiana General Assembly") and have dominated the [governorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Indiana "Governor of Indiana") since 2005.

## Culture

### Arts

See also: [Golden Age of Indiana Literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Age_of_Indiana_Literature "Golden Age of Indiana Literature")

The last decades of the 19th century began what is known as the "golden age of Indiana literature", a period that lasted until the 1920s.[[207]](#cite_note-artsummary-216) [Edward Eggleston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Eggleston "Edward Eggleston") wrote *The Hoosier Schoolmaster* (1871), the first best-seller to originate in the state. Many more followed, including [Maurice Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Thompson "Maurice Thompson")'s *Hoosier Mosaics* (1875) and [Lew Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lew_Wallace "Lew Wallace")'s *[Ben-Hur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben-Hur:_A_Tale_of_the_Christ "Ben-Hur: A Tale of the Christ")* (1880). Indiana developed a reputation as the "American heartland" after the publication of several widely read novels, beginning with [Booth Tarkington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Booth_Tarkington "Booth Tarkington")'s *The Gentleman from Indiana* (1899), [Meredith Nicholson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meredith_Nicholson "Meredith Nicholson")'s The Hoosiers (1900), and Thompson's *Alice of Old Vincennes* (1900).[[207]](#cite_note-artsummary-216) [James Whitcomb Riley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Whitcomb_Riley "James Whitcomb Riley"), known as the "Hoosier Poet" and the most popular poet of his age, wrote hundreds of poems with Hoosier themes, including *[Little Orphant Annie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Orphant_Annie "Little Orphant Annie")*. A unique art culture also began to develop in the late 19th century, beginning the [Hoosier School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoosier_Group "Hoosier Group") of landscape painting and the [Richmond Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richmond_Group "Richmond Group") of impressionist painters. The painters, including [T. C. Steele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T._C._Steele "T. C. Steele"), whose work was influenced by southern Indiana's colorful hills, were known for their use of vivid colors.[[207]](#cite_note-artsummary-216) Prominent musicians and composers from Indiana also reached national acclaim, including [Paul Dresser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Dresser "Paul Dresser"), whose most popular song, "[On the Banks of the Wabash, Far Away](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Banks_of_the_Wabash,_Far_Away "On the Banks of the Wabash, Far Away")", was later adopted as the official state song.[[208]](#cite_note-ihspauldresser-217)

### Sports

Main article: [Sports in Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_in_Indiana "Sports in Indiana")

#### Motorsports

[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Dallara_DW12_%28Fernando_Alonso%29_2017_Indianapolis_500.jpg/250px-Dallara_DW12_%28Fernando_Alonso%29_2017_Indianapolis_500.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dallara_DW12_(Fernando_Alonso)_2017_Indianapolis_500.jpg)

Indianapolis is home to the annual [Indianapolis 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianapolis_500 "Indianapolis 500") race.

Indiana has an extensive history with [auto racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auto_racing "Auto racing"). Indianapolis hosts the [Indianapolis 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianapolis_500 "Indianapolis 500") mile race over [Memorial Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_Day "Memorial Day") weekend at the [Indianapolis Motor Speedway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianapolis_Motor_Speedway "Indianapolis Motor Speedway") every May. The name of the race is usually shortened to "Indy 500" and also goes by the nickname "The Greatest Spectacle in Racing".[[209]](#cite_note-indianapolismotorspeedway.com-218) The race attracts more than 350,000 people every year, making it the largest single-day sporting event in the world.[[210]](#cite_note-219) The track also hosts the [Brickyard 400](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brickyard_400 "Brickyard 400") ([NASCAR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASCAR "NASCAR")) and the [Red Bull Indianapolis Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Bull_Indianapolis_Grand_Prix "Red Bull Indianapolis Grand Prix").[[209]](#cite_note-indianapolismotorspeedway.com-218) From 2000 to 2007, it hosted the [United States Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Grand_Prix "United States Grand Prix") ([Formula One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_One "Formula One")). Indiana features the world's largest and most prestigious [drag race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drag_race "Drag race"), the [NHRA Mac Tools U.S. Nationals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHRA_U.S._Nationals "NHRA U.S. Nationals"), held each [Labor Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_Day "Labor Day") weekend at [Lucas Oil Raceway at Indianapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucas_Oil_Raceway_at_Indianapolis "Lucas Oil Raceway at Indianapolis") in [Clermont, Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clermont,_Indiana "Clermont, Indiana"). Indiana is also host to a major [unlimited hydroplane racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroplane_racing "Hydroplane racing") power boat race circuits in the major [H1 Unlimited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H1_Unlimited "H1 Unlimited") league, the [Madison Regatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison_Regatta "Madison Regatta") ([Madison, Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison,_Indiana "Madison, Indiana")).[[211]](#cite_note-220)

#### Professional sports

[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Andrew_Luck_2013.jpg/250px-Andrew_Luck_2013.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Andrew_Luck_2013.jpg)

The [Indianapolis Colts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianapolis_Colts "Indianapolis Colts") of the [National Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Football_League "National Football League") have been based in the state since 1984.

As of 2013[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Indiana&action=edit) Indiana has produced more [National Basketball Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Basketball_Association "National Basketball Association") (NBA) players per capita than any other state. Muncie has produced the most per capita of any American city, with two other Indiana cities in the top ten.[[212]](#cite_note-deadspin20130617-221) It has a rich basketball heritage that reaches back to the sport's formative years. The NBA's [Indiana Pacers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Pacers "Indiana Pacers") play their home games at [Gainbridge Fieldhouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gainbridge_Fieldhouse "Gainbridge Fieldhouse"); they began play in 1967 in the [American Basketball Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Basketball_Association "American Basketball Association") (ABA) and joined the NBA when the leagues [merged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABA-NBA_merger "ABA-NBA merger") in 1976. Although [James Naismith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Naismith "James Naismith") developed basketball in [Springfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springfield,_Massachusetts "Springfield, Massachusetts"), [Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts "Massachusetts") in 1891, high school basketball was born in Indiana. In 1925, Naismith visited an Indiana basketball state finals game along with 15,000 screaming fans and later wrote "Basketball really had its origin in Indiana, which remains the center of the sport." The 1986 film *[Hoosiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoosiers_(film) "Hoosiers (film)")* is inspired by the story of the 1954 Indiana state champions [Milan High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Milan_High_School_basketball_team "1954 Milan High School basketball team"). Professional basketball player [Larry Bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Bird "Larry Bird") was born in [West Baden Springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Baden_Springs,_Indiana "West Baden Springs, Indiana") and was raised in [French Lick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Lick,_Indiana "French Lick, Indiana"). He went on to lead the [Boston Celtics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Celtics "Boston Celtics") to the NBA championship in 1981, 1984, and 1986.[[213]](#cite_note-222)

Indianapolis is home to the [Indianapolis Colts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianapolis_Colts "Indianapolis Colts"). The Colts are members of the [South Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFC_South "AFC South") of the [American Football Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Football_Conference "American Football Conference"). The Colts have roots back to 1913 as the [Dayton Triangles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dayton_Triangles "Dayton Triangles"). They became an official team after moving to [Baltimore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore "Baltimore"), [MD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maryland "Maryland"), in 1953. In 1984, the [Colts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Baltimore_Colts "History of the Baltimore Colts") [relocated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore_Colts_relocation_controversy "Baltimore Colts relocation controversy") to Indianapolis, leading to an eventual rivalry with the [Baltimore Ravens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore_Ravens "Baltimore Ravens"). After calling the [RCA Dome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RCA_Dome "RCA Dome") home for 25 years, the Colts play their home games at [Lucas Oil Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucas_Oil_Stadium "Lucas Oil Stadium") in Indianapolis. While in Baltimore, the Colts won [Super Bowl V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_V "Super Bowl V"). In Indianapolis, the Colts won [Super Bowl XLI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_XLI "Super Bowl XLI"), bringing the franchise total to two. In recent years the Colts have regularly competed in the NFL playoffs.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

Indiana was home to two charter members of the [National Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Football_League "National Football League") teams, the [Hammond Pros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammond_Pros "Hammond Pros") and the [Muncie Flyers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muncie_Flyers "Muncie Flyers"). Another early NFL franchise, the [Evansville Crimson Giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evansville_Crimson_Giants "Evansville Crimson Giants") spent two seasons in the league before folding.[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

#### College athletics

See also: [Hoosier Hysteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoosier_Hysteria "Hoosier Hysteria")

[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/Indiana_Assembly_Hall_interior_%2816100187018%29.jpg/250px-Indiana_Assembly_Hall_interior_%2816100187018%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Indiana_Assembly_Hall_interior_(16100187018).jpg)

[Simon Skjodt Assembly Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Skjodt_Assembly_Hall "Simon Skjodt Assembly Hall"), home to [Indiana Hoosiers men's basketball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Hoosiers_men's_basketball "Indiana Hoosiers men's basketball")

 [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Fans_and_players_gather_for_a_football_game_Sept._6%2C_2014%2C_at_Notre_Dame_Stadium_in_South_Bend%2C_Ind_140906-D-KC128-220.jpg/250px-Fans_and_players_gather_for_a_football_game_Sept._6%2C_2014%2C_at_Notre_Dame_Stadium_in_South_Bend%2C_Ind_140906-D-KC128-220.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Fans_and_players_gather_for_a_football_game_Sept._6,_2014,_at_Notre_Dame_Stadium_in_South_Bend,_Ind_140906-D-KC128-220.jpg)

[Notre Dame Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Stadium "Notre Dame Stadium"), home to the [Fighting Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish_football "Notre Dame Fighting Irish football")

 [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Ross-Ade_Stadium.jpg/250px-Ross-Ade_Stadium.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ross-Ade_Stadium.jpg)

[Ross-Ade Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ross-Ade_Stadium "Ross-Ade Stadium"), home of the [Purdue Boilermakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purdue_Boilermakers_football "Purdue Boilermakers football"), before the 2023 renovations

Eleven Indiana universities participate in [NCAA Division I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I "NCAA Division I") athletics, winning league and national championships over the years.

In men's basketball, the [Indiana Hoosiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Hoosiers_men's_basketball "Indiana Hoosiers men's basketball") have won five NCAA national championships and 22 [Big Ten Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Ten_Conference "Big Ten Conference") championships. The [Purdue Boilermakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purdue_Boilermakers "Purdue Boilermakers") were selected as the national champions in 1932 before the creation of the tournament, and have won 26 Big Ten championships. The Boilermakers along with the Notre Dame Fighting Irish have both won a national championship in women's basketball.

In college football, the [Notre Dame Fighting Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Fighting_Irish "Notre Dame Fighting Irish") have won 11 consensus national championships, as well as the [Rose Bowl Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_Bowl_Game "Rose Bowl Game"), [Cotton Bowl Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton_Bowl_Classic "Cotton Bowl Classic"), [Orange Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Bowl "Orange Bowl") and [Sugar Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_Bowl "Sugar Bowl"). Meanwhile, the Purdue Boilermakers have won 10 Big Ten championships and have won the Rose Bowl and [Peach Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peach_Bowl "Peach Bowl"). The [Indiana Hoosiers football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Indiana_Hoosiers_football_team "2025 Indiana Hoosiers football team"), led by head coach [Curt Cignetti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curt_Cignetti "Curt Cignetti"), won the [national championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_College_Football_Playoff_National_Championship "2026 College Football Playoff National Championship") in 2026.

Schools fielding NCAA Division I athletic programs include:

## Economy and infrastructure

Main article: [Economy of Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Indiana "Economy of Indiana")

[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Indiana_Dunes_State_Park%2C_on_the_southern_tip_of_Lake_Michigan.jpg/250px-Indiana_Dunes_State_Park%2C_on_the_southern_tip_of_Lake_Michigan.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Indiana_Dunes_State_Park,_on_the_southern_tip_of_Lake_Michigan.jpg)

[Lake Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Michigan "Lake Michigan")'s beaches, popular with tourists, are juxtaposed with heavy industry.

 [![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/Campo_de_maiz%2C_Walker%2C_Indiana%2C_Estados_Unidos%2C_2012-10-20%2C_DD_03.jpg/250px-Campo_de_maiz%2C_Walker%2C_Indiana%2C_Estados_Unidos%2C_2012-10-20%2C_DD_03.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Campo_de_maiz,_Walker,_Indiana,_Estados_Unidos,_2012-10-20,_DD_03.jpg)

Indiana is the fifth largest corn-producing state in the U.S., with over a billion bushels harvested in 2013.[[214]](#cite_note-223)

In 2017, Indiana had a civilian labor force of nearly 3.4 million, the 15th largest in the United States. Indiana has an unemployment rate of 3.7%, as of May 2025.[[215]](#cite_note-224) The total gross state product in 2016 was $347.2 billion.[[216]](#cite_note-225) A high percentage of Indiana's income is from manufacturing.[[217]](#cite_note-226) According to the [Bureau of Labor Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Labor_Statistics "Bureau of Labor Statistics"), nearly 17% of the state's non-farm workforce is employed in manufacturing, the highest of any state in the U.S.[[218]](#cite_note-227) The state's five leading exports were motor vehicles and auto parts, pharmaceutical products, industrial machinery, optical and medical equipment, and electric machinery.[[219]](#cite_note-228)

Despite its reliance on manufacturing, Indiana has been less affected by declines in traditional [Rust Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rust_Belt "Rust Belt") manufacturers than many of its neighbors. The explanation appears to be certain factors in the labor market. First, much of the heavy manufacturing, such as industrial machinery and steel, requires highly skilled labor, and firms are often willing to locate where hard-to-train skills already exist. Second, Indiana's labor force is primarily in medium-sized and smaller cities rather than in very large and expensive metropolises. This makes it possible for firms to offer somewhat lower wages for these skills than would normally be paid. Firms often see in Indiana a chance to obtain higher than average skills at lower than average wages.[[220]](#cite_note-229)

Indiana is in the U.S. [Corn Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corn_Belt "Corn Belt") and [Grain Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grain_Belt "Grain Belt"). It has a feedlot-style system raising corn to fatten hogs and cattle. Along with corn, [soybeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soybeans "Soybeans") are also a major cash crop. Its proximity to large urban centers, such as [Indianapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianapolis "Indianapolis") and Chicago, assure dairying, egg production, and specialty horticulture occur. Other crops include melons, tomatoes, grapes, mint, popping corn, and tobacco in the southern counties.[[221]](#cite_note-230) Most of the original land was not prairie and had to be cleared of deciduous trees. Many parcels of woodland remain and support a furniture-making sector in southern Indiana.

### Business

In 2025, Indiana was home to seven [Fortune 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_500 "Fortune 500") companies. These included [Eli Lilly and Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eli_Lilly_and_Company "Eli Lilly and Company"), the pharmaceutical giant and the state's largest corporation, headquartered in Indianapolis.[[222]](#cite_note-Beard-2025-231) Indiana ranks fifth among all U.S. states in total sales and shipments of pharmaceutical products and second in the number of biopharmaceutical related jobs.[[223]](#cite_note-232)

[Northwest Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Indiana "Northwest Indiana") has been the largest steel producing center in the U.S. since 1975,[[224]](#cite_note-233) with Fort Wayne hosting Fortune 500 [Steel Dynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_Dynamics "Steel Dynamics"), the third largest producer of carbon steel products in the United States.

Newer manufacturing sectors include orthopedic and surgical products, represented by [Zimmer Biomet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zimmer_Biomet "Zimmer Biomet"), based in [Warsaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw,_Indiana "Warsaw, Indiana"). Among the major service providers are [Elevance Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elevance_Health "Elevance Health") (formerly Anthem, Inc.) in Indianapolis which is ranked among the nation's top healthcare insurers.[[222]](#cite_note-Beard-2025-231)

In 2025, small businesses (generally defined as those with under 500 employees) accounted for 43 percent of Indiana's employees. While the number of small businesses has increased since the end of the COVID pandemic in 2022, it has done so without a commensurate increase in employment. Many are sole traders and self-employed contractors.[[225]](#cite_note-234)[[226]](#cite_note-235)

Evaluating factors such as the private sector business creation rate, patent grants, tax burden, venture capital investment and how many top businesses are headquartered in a state, in 2025, *US News & World Report* ranked Indiana 39th among the 50 states for its "business environment".[[86]](#cite_note-USWR_Best_State_Rankings_2025-89)

### Taxation

See also: [Taxation in Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_Indiana "Taxation in Indiana")

Tax is collected by the [Indiana Department of Revenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Department_of_Revenue "Indiana Department of Revenue").[[227]](#cite_note-236)

Indiana has a flat state [income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax "Income tax") rate of 3.23%. Many of the state's counties also collect income tax. The state [sales tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sales_tax "Sales tax") rate is 7% with exemptions for food, prescription medications and over-the-counter medications.[[228]](#cite_note-237) In some jurisdictions, an additional Food and Beverage Tax is charged, at a rate of 1% (Marion County's rate is 2%), on sales of prepared meals and beverages.[[229]](#cite_note-238)

[Property taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_tax "Property tax") are imposed on both real and personal property in Indiana and are administered by the Department of Local Government Finance.[[230]](#cite_note-239) Property is subject to taxation by a variety of taxing units (schools, counties, townships, municipalities, and libraries), making the total tax rate the sum of the tax rates imposed by all taxing units in which a property is located. However, a "circuit breaker" law enacted on March 19, 2008, limits property taxes to 1% of assessed value for homeowners, 2% for rental properties and farmland, and 3% for businesses.

### State budget

Indiana does not have a legal requirement to balance the state budget either in law or its constitution. Instead, it has a constitutional ban on assuming debt. The state has a [Rainy Day Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainy_Day_Fund "Rainy Day Fund") and for healthy reserves proportional to spending. Indiana is one of six U.S. states to not allow a [line-item veto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line-item_veto_in_the_United_States "Line-item veto in the United States").[[231]](#cite_note-240)

Since 2010, Indiana has been one of a few states to hold [AAA bond credit ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bond_credit_rating#Credit_rating_tiers "Bond credit rating") with the [Big Three](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Three_(credit_rating_agencies) "Big Three (credit rating agencies)") credit rating agencies, the highest possible rating.[[232]](#cite_note-241)

### Energy

See also: [List of power stations in Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_power_stations_in_Indiana "List of power stations in Indiana")

[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/Clifty_Creek_Power_Plant_Complex.jpg/250px-Clifty_Creek_Power_Plant_Complex.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Clifty_Creek_Power_Plant_Complex.jpg)

[Coal-fired electric plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel_power_station "Fossil fuel power station"), like [Clifty Creek Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clifty_Creek_Power_Plant "Clifty Creek Power Plant") in [Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison,_Indiana "Madison, Indiana"), produced about 85 percent of Indiana's energy supply in 2014.[[233]](#cite_note-242)

Indiana's power production chiefly consists of the consumption of fossil fuels, mainly coal. It has 24 coal power plants, including the country's largest coal power plant, [Gibson Generating Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gibson_Generating_Station "Gibson Generating Station"), across the Wabash River from [Mount Carmel, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Carmel,_Illinois "Mount Carmel, Illinois"). Indiana was also home to the coal-fired plant with the highest sulfur dioxide emissions in the United States, the [Gallagher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R._Gallagher_Generating_Station "R. Gallagher Generating Station") power plant, just west of New Albany.[[234]](#cite_note-243)

In 2010, Indiana had estimated coal reserves of 57 billion tons, and state mining operations produced 35 million tons of coal annually.[[235]](#cite_note-244) Indiana also has at least 900 million barrels of petroleum reserves in the [Trenton Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trenton_Gas_Field "Trenton Gas Field"), though they are not easily recoverable. While Indiana has made commitments to increasing the use of renewable resources such as wind, hydroelectric, biomass, or solar power, progress has been very slow, mainly because of the continued abundance of coal in southern Indiana. Most of the new plants in the state have been [coal gasification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_gasification "Coal gasification") plants. Another source is hydroelectric power.

[Wind power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power_in_Indiana "Wind power in Indiana") has been growing rapidly. Estimates in 2006 raised Indiana's wind capacity from 30 MW at 50 m turbine height to 40,000 MW at 70 m, and to 130,000 MW at 100 m, in 2010, the height of newer turbines.[[236]](#cite_note-245) By the end of 2011, Indiana had installed 1,340 MW of wind turbines.[[237]](#cite_note-246) In 2020, this total had more than doubled to 2,968 MW.[[238]](#cite_note-247)

### Transportation

#### Airports

See also: [List of airports in Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Indiana "List of airports in Indiana")

[Indianapolis International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianapolis_International_Airport "Indianapolis International Airport") serves the greater Indianapolis area. It was rebuilt in November 2008 and offers a midfield passenger terminal, concourses, air traffic control tower, parking garage, and airfield and apron improvements.[[239]](#cite_note-248)

Other major airports include [Evansville Regional Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evansville_Regional_Airport "Evansville Regional Airport"), [Fort Wayne International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Wayne_International_Airport "Fort Wayne International Airport") (which houses the [122d Fighter Wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/122d_Fighter_Wing "122d Fighter Wing") of the [Air National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_National_Guard "Air National Guard")), and [South Bend International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Bend_International_Airport "South Bend International Airport"). A long-standing proposal to turn [Gary Chicago International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Chicago_International_Airport "Gary Chicago International Airport") into Chicago's third major airport received a boost in early 2006 with the approval of $48 million in federal funding over the next ten years.[[240]](#cite_note-249)

No airlines operate out of [Terre Haute Regional Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terre_Haute_Regional_Airport "Terre Haute Regional Airport") but it is used primarily for general aviation. Since 1954, the [181st Fighter Wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/181st_Fighter_Wing "181st Fighter Wing") of the Indiana [Air National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_National_Guard "Air National Guard") was stationed there, but the [Base Realignment and Closure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Base_Realignment_and_Closure "Base Realignment and Closure") (BRAC) Proposal of 2005 stated the 181st would lose its fighter mission and [F-16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-16 "F-16") aircraft, leaving the Terre Haute facility a general-aviation-only facility.

[Louisville International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisville_International_Airport "Louisville International Airport"), across the Ohio River in [Louisville, Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisville,_Kentucky "Louisville, Kentucky"), serves southern Indiana, as does [Cincinnati/Northern Kentucky International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati/Northern_Kentucky_International_Airport "Cincinnati/Northern Kentucky International Airport") in [Hebron, Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebron,_Kentucky "Hebron, Kentucky"). Many residents of [Northwest Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Indiana "Northwest Indiana"), which is primarily in the [Chicago Metropolitan Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Metropolitan_Area "Chicago Metropolitan Area"), use Chicago's airports, [O'Hare International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O'Hare_International_Airport "O'Hare International Airport") and [Chicago Midway International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Midway_International_Airport "Chicago Midway International Airport").[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

#### Highways

[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/I-69_Construction_Indiana.jpg/250px-I-69_Construction_Indiana.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:I-69_Construction_Indiana.jpg)

The [Interstate 69](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_69_in_Indiana "Interstate 69 in Indiana") extension project in [Monroe County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe_County,_Indiana "Monroe County, Indiana")

The U.S. [Interstate highways in Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Interstate_Highways_in_Indiana "List of Interstate Highways in Indiana") are [I-64](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_64_in_Indiana "Interstate 64 in Indiana"), [I-65](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_65_in_Indiana "Interstate 65 in Indiana"), [I-265](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_265_in_Indiana "Interstate 265 in Indiana"), [I-465](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_465 "Interstate 465"), [I-865](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_865 "Interstate 865"), [I-69](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_69_in_Indiana "Interstate 69 in Indiana"), [I-469](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_469 "Interstate 469"), [I-70](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_70_in_Indiana "Interstate 70 in Indiana"), [I-74](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_74_in_Indiana "Interstate 74 in Indiana"), [I-80](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_80_in_Indiana "Interstate 80 in Indiana"), [I-90](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_90_in_Indiana "Interstate 90 in Indiana"), [I-94](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_94_in_Indiana "Interstate 94 in Indiana"), and [I-275](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_275_(Ohio–Indiana–Kentucky) "Interstate 275 (Ohio–Indiana–Kentucky)"). The various highways intersecting in and around [Indianapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianapolis "Indianapolis"), along with its historical status as a major railroad hub, and the canals that once crossed Indiana, are the source of the state's motto, the Crossroads of America. There are also many [U.S. routes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._Routes_in_Indiana "List of U.S. Routes in Indiana") and [state highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_State_Roads_in_Indiana "List of State Roads in Indiana") maintained by the [Indiana Department of Transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Department_of_Transportation "Indiana Department of Transportation"). These are numbered according to the same convention as [U.S. Highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Highways "U.S. Highways"). Indiana allows highways of different classifications to have the same number. For example, I-64 and [Indiana State Road 64](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_State_Road_64 "Indiana State Road 64") both exist (rather close to each other) in Indiana, but are two distinct roads with no relation to one another.

A $4.3 billion [project extending I-69](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_69_in_Indiana#History "Interstate 69 in Indiana") an additional 142 miles (229 km) from Evansville to Indianapolis was completed in 2024.[[241]](#cite_note-250)

#### County roads

Most Indiana counties use a grid-based system to identify county roads; this system replaced the older arbitrary system of road numbers and names, and (among other things) makes it much easier to identify the sources of calls placed to the [9-1-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9-1-1 "9-1-1") system. Such systems are easier to implement in the glacially flattened northern and central portions of the state. Rural counties in the southern third of the state are less likely to have grids and more likely to rely on unsystematic road names (for example, Crawford, Harrison, Perry, Scott, and Washington Counties).

There are also counties in the northern portions of the state that have never implemented a grid or have only partially implemented one. Some counties are also laid out in an almost diamond-like grid system (e.g., Clark, Floyd, Gibson, and Knox Counties). Such a system is also almost useless in those situations as well. Knox County once operated two different grid systems for county roads because the county was laid out using two different survey grids, but has since decided to use road names and combine roads instead.

Notably, the county road grid system of St. Joseph County, whose major city is South Bend, uses perennial (tree) names (i.e. Ash, Hickory, Ironwood, etc.) in alphabetical order for north–south roads and presidential and other noteworthy names (i.e., Adams, Edison, Lincoln Way, etc.) in alphabetical order for east–west roads. There are exceptions to this rule in downtown South Bend and Mishawaka. Hamilton County's east–west roads continue Indianapolis's numbered street system from 96th Street at the Marion County line to 296th street at the Tipton County line.

#### Rail

See also: [List of Indiana railroads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indiana_railroads "List of Indiana railroads")

[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2a/07_21_09_006xRP_-_Flickr_-_drewj1946.jpg/250px-07_21_09_006xRP_-_Flickr_-_drewj1946.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:07_21_09_006xRP_-_Flickr_-_drewj1946.jpg)

A [South Shore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Shore_Line "South Shore Line") commuter train in [Michigan City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan_City,_Indiana "Michigan City, Indiana")

Indiana has more than 4,255 railroad route miles (6,848 km), of which 91% are operated by [Class I railroads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_I_railroad "Class I railroad"), principally [CSX Transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSX_Transportation "CSX Transportation") and the [Norfolk Southern Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norfolk_Southern_Railway "Norfolk Southern Railway"). Other Class I railroads in Indiana include the [Canadian National Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_National_Railway "Canadian National Railway") and [Soo Line Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soo_Line_Railroad "Soo Line Railroad"), a [CPKC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Pacific_Kansas_City "Canadian Pacific Kansas City") subsidiary, as well as [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak "Amtrak"). The remaining miles are operated by 37 regional, local, and switching and terminal railroads. The [South Shore Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Shore_Line_(NICTD) "South Shore Line (NICTD)") is one of the country's most notable commuter rail systems, extending from Chicago to [South Bend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Bend "South Bend"). Indiana is implementing an extensive rail plan prepared in 2002 by the [Parsons Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsons_Corporation "Parsons Corporation").[[242]](#cite_note-251) Many recreational trails, such as the [Monon Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monon_Trail "Monon Trail") and [Cardinal Greenway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardinal_Greenway "Cardinal Greenway"), have been created from [abandoned rails routes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rails-to-trails "Rails-to-trails").

#### Ports

[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Towboat_Ben_McCool_upbound_on_Ohio_River_with_two_tank_barges_%281_of_6%29_87j082.jpg/250px-Towboat_Ben_McCool_upbound_on_Ohio_River_with_two_tank_barges_%281_of_6%29_87j082.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Towboat_Ben_McCool_upbound_on_Ohio_River_with_two_tank_barges_(1_of_6)_87j082.jpg)

Barges are a common sight along the [Ohio River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_River "Ohio River"). Ports of Indiana manages three maritime ports in the state, two located on the Ohio.

Indiana annually ships more than 70 million tons of cargo by water each year, which ranks 14th among all U.S. states.[[243]](#cite_note-252) More than half of Indiana's border is water, which includes 400 miles (640 km) of direct access to two major freight transportation arteries: the Great Lakes/St. Lawrence Seaway (via Lake Michigan) and the Inland Waterway System (via the Ohio River). The [Ports of Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Indiana "Port of Indiana") manages three major ports which include [Burns Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burns_Harbor,_Indiana "Burns Harbor, Indiana"), [Jeffersonville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffersonville,_Indiana "Jeffersonville, Indiana"), and [Mount Vernon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Vernon,_Indiana "Mount Vernon, Indiana").[[244]](#cite_note-253)

## Education

Main article: [Education in Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Indiana "Education in Indiana")

### Public schools

[![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Shortridge_High_School_Indianapolis_Aug_2016.jpg/250px-Shortridge_High_School_Indianapolis_Aug_2016.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Shortridge_High_School_Indianapolis_Aug_2016.jpg)

Established in 1864, [Shortridge High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortridge_High_School "Shortridge High School") in Indianapolis is Indiana's oldest free public high school.[[245]](#cite_note-254)

Indiana's 1816 constitution was the first in the country to implement a state-funded [public school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_school_(government_funded) "Public school (government funded)") system. It also allotted one township for a public university.[[246]](#cite_note-255) However, the plan turned out to be far too idealistic for a pioneer society, as tax money was not accessible for its organization. In the 1840s, [Caleb Mills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caleb_Mills "Caleb Mills") pressed the need for tax-supported schools, and in 1851 his advice was included in the new state constitution. In 1843 the Legislature ruled that African Americans could not attend the public schools, leading to the foundation of [Union Literary Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Literary_Institute "Union Literary Institute") and other schools for them, funded by donations or the students themselves.[[247]](#cite_note-cepr.indiana.edu-256) The Indiana General Assembly authorized separate but equal schools for Black students in 1869, and in 1877 language in the law changed to allow for integrated schools.[[247]](#cite_note-cepr.indiana.edu-256)

Although the growth of the public school system was held up by legal entanglements, many public elementary schools were in use by 1870. Most children in Indiana attend public schools, but nearly ten percent attend private schools and [parochial schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parochial_school "Parochial school").[[248]](#cite_note-257) About half of all college students in Indiana are enrolled in state-supported four-year schools.

Indiana public schools have gone through several changes throughout Indiana's history. Modern, public school standards have been implemented all throughout the state. These new standards were adopted in April 2014. The overall goal of these new state standards is to ensure Indiana students have the necessary skills and requirements needed to enter college or the workforce upon high school graduation.[[249]](#cite_note-258) State standards can be found for nearly every major subject taught in Indiana public schools. Mathematics, English/Language Arts, Science, and Social Studies are among the top, prioritized standards. In 2022, the [Indiana Department of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Department_of_Education "Indiana Department of Education") reported that the state's overall graduation rate was 86.7%, down one percent from 2021.[[250]](#cite_note-259)

The rate of Indiana high school students attending college fell to 53% in 2022, a significant decline from 65% in 2017.[[251]](#cite_note-Chalkbeat_Indiana-2022-260)[[252]](#cite_note-261) Indiana's college-going rates have fallen further than most states'.[[253]](#cite_note-262)[[251]](#cite_note-Chalkbeat_Indiana-2022-260)[[254]](#cite_note-263) Trends reveal widening gaps for ethnic minorities and low-income families.[[251]](#cite_note-Chalkbeat_Indiana-2022-260)

### Vocational schools

Indiana has a strong vocational school system. [Charles Allen Prossor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Allen_Prossor "Charles Allen Prossor"), known as the father of [vocational education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocational_education "Vocational education") in the United States, was from [New Albany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Albany,_Indiana "New Albany, Indiana"). The [Charles Allen Prosser School of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Allen_Prosser_School_of_Technology "Charles Allen Prosser School of Technology") is named in his honor. There are vocational schools in every region of Indiana, and most Indiana students can freely attend a vocational school during their high school years and receive training and job placement assistance in trade jobs. The International Union Of Operating Engineers (IUOE) has seven local unions in Indiana, offering apprenticeship and training opportunities.[[255]](#cite_note-264) According to the Electrical Training Alliance website, there are ten electrical training centers in Indiana.[[256]](#cite_note-265)

### Colleges and universities

See also: [List of colleges and universities in Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Indiana "List of colleges and universities in Indiana")

The state's community college system, [Ivy Tech Community College of Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivy_Tech_Community_College_of_Indiana "Ivy Tech Community College of Indiana"), serves nearly 200,000 students annually, making it the state's largest public post-secondary educational institution and the nation's largest singly accredited statewide community college system.[[257]](#cite_note-266) In 2008, the Indiana University system agreed to shift most of its associate (2-year) degrees to the Ivy Tech Community College System.[[258]](#cite_note-267)

The largest non-community educational institution is [Indiana University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_University "Indiana University"), a multi-campus university system; its [flagship campus at Bloomington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_University_Bloomington "Indiana University Bloomington") was endorsed as the Indiana Seminary in 1820. [Indiana State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_State_University "Indiana State University") was established in Terre Haute as the state's [Normal School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normal_School "Normal School") in 1865. [Purdue University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purdue_University "Purdue University") was chartered in West Lafayette as the state's [land-grant university](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land-grant_university "Land-grant university") in 1869 and is also now a multi-campus institution. The three other independent state universities are [Vincennes University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vincennes_University "Vincennes University") (founded in 1801 by the Indiana Territory), [Ball State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ball_State_University "Ball State University") (founded 1918 as the East Division of Indiana State), and the [University of Southern Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Southern_Indiana "University of Southern Indiana") (founded 1965 as the Evansville campus of Indiana State).

Many of Indiana's private colleges and universities are affiliated with religious organizations. The [University of Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Notre_Dame "University of Notre Dame"), [Marian University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marian_University_(Indiana) "Marian University (Indiana)"), and the [University of Saint Francis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Saint_Francis_(Indiana) "University of Saint Francis (Indiana)") are [Roman Catholic schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_school "Roman Catholic school"). Universities affiliated with Protestant denominations include [Anderson University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anderson_University_(Indiana) "Anderson University (Indiana)"), [Butler University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butler_University "Butler University"), [Huntington University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huntington_University_(United_States) "Huntington University (United States)"), [Manchester University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_University_(Indiana) "Manchester University (Indiana)"), [Indiana Wesleyan University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Wesleyan_University "Indiana Wesleyan University"), [Taylor University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taylor_University "Taylor University"), [Franklin College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_College_(Indiana) "Franklin College (Indiana)"), [Hanover College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanover_College "Hanover College"), [DePauw University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DePauw_University "DePauw University"), [Earlham College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earlham_College "Earlham College"), [Valparaiso University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valparaiso_University "Valparaiso University"), the [University of Indianapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Indianapolis "University of Indianapolis"),[[182]](#cite_note-ST-191) and the [University of Evansville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Evansville "University of Evansville").[[259]](#cite_note-268)

The state has several universities ranked among the best by *[U.S. News & World Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._News_&_World_Report "U.S. News & World Report")*. The University of Notre Dame ranks among the top 20, Purdue University among the top 50, and Indiana University Bloomington among the top 100.[[260]](#cite_note-269)[[261]](#cite_note-270)[[262]](#cite_note-271) The three former schools are all [R1 Research Institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R1_institution "R1 institution"), along with [Indiana University Indianapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_University_Indianapolis "Indiana University Indianapolis") from the former [Indiana University–Purdue University Indianapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_University–Purdue_University_Indianapolis "Indiana University–Purdue University Indianapolis").[[263]](#cite_note-272) Butler, Valparaiso, and the University of Evansville are ranked among the top ten in the Regional University Midwest Rankings. Purdue's engineering programs are ranked fourth in the country.[[264]](#cite_note-273) In addition, Taylor University is ranked first in the Regional College Midwest Rankings and [Rose-Hulman Institute of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose-Hulman_Institute_of_Technology "Rose-Hulman Institute of Technology") has been considered the nation's top undergraduate engineering school for 25 consecutive years.[[265]](#cite_note-274)[[266]](#cite_note-275)[[267]](#cite_note-276)[[268]](#cite_note-277)[[269]](#cite_note-278) In 2023, the University of Notre Dame had the [seventh largest endowment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_the_United_States_by_endowment "List of colleges and universities in the United States by endowment") among private postsecondary institutions in the U.S. (11th overall).

The state is also home to the largest [medical school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_school_in_the_United_States "Medical school in the United States") system in the country (the [Indiana University School of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_University_School_of_Medicine "Indiana University School of Medicine")) and a smaller, [osteopathic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osteopathic_medicine_in_the_United_States "Osteopathic medicine in the United States") medical school (Marian University's [Tom and Julie Wood College of Osteopathic Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_and_Julie_Wood_College_of_Osteopathic_Medicine "Tom and Julie Wood College of Osteopathic Medicine")). In addition, Indiana boasts one [veterinary medical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veterinary_medicine "Veterinary medicine") school (the Purdue College of Veterinary Medicine), one [optometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optometry "Optometry") school (Indiana University School of Optometry), three [pharmacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmacy "Pharmacy") schools (the [Purdue College of Pharmacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purdue_University_College_of_Pharmacy "Purdue University College of Pharmacy"), Butler College of Pharmacy and Health Sciences, and the Manchester College of Pharmacy, Natural, and Health Sciences) and four [law schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_school "Law school") ([IU Maurer School of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_University_Maurer_School_of_Law "Indiana University Maurer School of Law"), [IU McKinney School of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_University_Robert_H._McKinney_School_of_Law "Indiana University Robert H. McKinney School of Law"), [Notre Dame Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Law_School "Notre Dame Law School"), and [Purdue Global Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purdue_Global_Law_School "Purdue Global Law School")).

Indianapolis: Indiana Historical Bureau and the Indiana Historical Society. pp. 311–13, 337, 353, 355, 432.

[↑](#cite_ref-118) In a 2008 report, Indiana was listed as one of the most tornado-prone states, ranking sixth, while South Bend was ranked the 14th most tornado-prone U.S. city, ahead of cities such as [Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston "Houston"), Texas, and [Wichita, Kansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wichita,_Kansas "Wichita, Kansas"). See Mecklenburg, Rick (May 1, 2008). ["Is Indiana the new Tornado Alley?"](https://web.archive.org/web/20130117084147/http://articles.southbendtribune.com/2008-05-01/news/26911876_1_tornado-track-damaging-winds-and-tornadoes-indiana). SouthBendTribune.com. Archived from [the original](http://articles.southbendtribune.com/2008-05-01/news/26911876_1_tornado-track-damaging-winds-and-tornadoes-indiana) on January 17, 2013. Retrieved August 13, 2012.
5. [↑](#cite_ref-119) In a published list of the most tornado-prone states and cities in April 2008, Indiana came in first and South Bend ranked 16th. See Henderson, Mark (May 2, 2008). ["Top 20 Tornado Prone Cities and States Announced"](https://web.archive.org/web/20081109034652/http://www.wifr.com/weather/headlines/17036536.html). [WIFR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WIFR "WIFR"). Archived from [the original](http://www.wifr.com/weather/headlines/17036536.html) on November 9, 2008. Retrieved August 17, 2009.
6. [↑](#cite_ref-127) A 2008 news report indicated there were 13 metropolitan areas in Indiana. See Dresang, Joel (July 30, 2008). ["Automaking down, unemployment up"](http://www.jsonline.com/blogs/business/52021282.html). *[Milwaukee Journal Sentinel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Journal_Sentinel "Milwaukee Journal Sentinel")*. Retrieved August 14, 2009.
7. [↑](#cite_ref-128) Indiana's territorial capitals were [Vincennes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vincennes,_Indiana "Vincennes, Indiana") and later [Corydon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corydon,_Indiana "Corydon, Indiana"), which also became Indiana's first [state capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_current_and_former_capital_cities_within_U.S._states "List of current and former capital cities within U.S. states") when it became a state.
8. [↑](#cite_ref-134) Over the previous decade, Indiana's population center has shifted slightly to the northwest. In the 2000 U.S. Census, Indiana's [center of population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_of_population "Center of population") was located in [Hamilton County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamilton_County,_Indiana "Hamilton County, Indiana"), in the town of [Sheridan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheridan,_Indiana "Sheridan, Indiana"). See ["Population and Population Centers by State"](https://web.archive.org/web/20130508041813/http://www.census.gov/geo/www/cenpop/statecenters.txt). United States Census Bureau. Archived from [the original](https://www.census.gov/geo/www/cenpop/statecenters.txt) on May 8, 2013. Retrieved November 21, 2006.
9. [↑](#cite_ref-142) Persons of Hispanic or Latino origin are not distinguished between total and partial ancestry.
